0<p<1,d\geq 0
. Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{N}
be random events, such that
\mathbb{P}(A_{i})\leq p
for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and that each
A_{i}
is independent of all but
d
other events. Suppose that
ep(d+1)<1
where
e=\exp(1)=2.71\ldots
is the Euler’s constant. Then, the probability that none of
A_{i}
occur is positive.

Proof of Theorem
4.8
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded geometry family of Riemannian
n
-manifolds and let
M\in\Omega
. Let
\delta>0
be a small positive parameter, to be fixed later. Choose
\varepsilon>0
so that the conclusion of Lemma
4.12
holds. We choose
0<r_{0}\leq 1
so that for any
r_{1}<r_{0}/20
the exponent map

\exp_{x}\colon T_{x}\to M
is an
(1+\varepsilon)
-isometry on the ball
\{v\in T_{x}M\mid\|v\|\leq 32r_{1}\},x\in M
and
\injrad(M)\geq 64r_{1}
.
We choose a maximal
4r_{1}
-separated set
P\subset M
. Then, by maximality,
P
is a
8r_{1}
-net.

We construct a new random set
\hat{P}
by independently replacing each
p\in P
by a uniformly randomly chosen element
\hat{p}\in B(p,r_{1}).
The new set
\hat{P}
is
2r_{1}
-separated
10r_{1}
-net.
We will say that a subset
E\subset\hat{P}
of
(n+1)
-points is
Delaunay
if

(1)

there is a unique minimal closed ball
B_{E}
containing
E
.

(2)

the ball
B_{E}
contains no other points of
\hat{P}.

Note that condition (1) is automatically satisfied for non-positively curved manifolds and subsets of diameter below the injectivity radius. Any lower cardinality subset
F\subset\hat{Q}
will be declared Delaunay if it is contained in some
(n+1)
-element Delaunay set.

Claim 1.
Let
E
be a Delaunay set. Then, the diameter of
E
is bounded by
20r_{1}
.

For the proof we may assume
|E|=n+1.
The minimal closed ball
B_{E}
containing
E
contains no other points so its radius is bounded by
10r_{1}
. Otherwise, the center of
B_{E}
would more than
10r_{1}
-away from
\hat{P}
, contradicting the fact that
\hat{P}
is a
10r_{1}
-net. The diameter of
E
is therefore bounded by
20r_{1}
, proving the claim.

For each Delaunay set
E
let
\sigma_{E}\subset M
be the simplex spanned by
E
. The exponential maps are
(1+\varepsilon)
-isometries on the balls of radius
32r_{1}
, which is bigger than the diameter of any Delaunay set. Hence, by Lemma
4.12
, each
\sigma_{E}
is
(1+\delta)
-isometric to an affine simplex. Write
{\rm Del}(\hat{P})
for the set of simplices in
M
spanned by the Delaunay subsets.

We will prove that for
\delta
small, with positive probability
{\rm Del}(\hat{P})
is an
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform triangulation of
M
. For that, we need to check three conditions.

(1)

the interiors of the simplices
\sigma_{F}
are pairwise disjoint.

(2)

the simplices
\sigma_{F}\in{\rm Del}(\hat{Q})
cover entire
M
.

(3)

every simplex of
{\rm Del}(\hat{Q})
is
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform.

For any
p\in P
(the original set, before the random resampling) let
A_{p}
be the event that one of the conditions (1) and (2) fails inside
B_{M}(p,8r_{1})
or that
B_{M}(p,28r_{1})
contains an
(n+1)
-tuple of points spanning a non-
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform simplex. Note that any non
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform simplex in
{\rm Del}(\hat{P})
would trigger at least one of the events
A_{p}
. Similarly, a failure to be a triangulation would also trigger some of the
A_{p}
’s, as
B_{M}(p,8r_{1}),p\in P
cover entire
M
. Therefore, to check if
{\rm Del}(\hat{P})
is an
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform triangulation of
M
it is enough to show that none of the
A_{p}
’s occur.
The bound on the diameter of
\sigma\in{\rm Del}(\hat{Q})
ensures that the events
A_{p},A_{q}
are independent if
d(p,q)>2(20+8+1)r_{1}=58r_{1}
and
p,q\in P
. The
8r_{1}
’s come from the radii,
20r_{1}
from the a priori diameter bounds and
r_{1}
’s from the resampling radius.

Since
P
is
4r_{1}
-separated
8r_{1}
-net, the size of the set
\{q\in P\mid d(p,q)\leq 58r_{1}\}
is bounded uniformly for all
p\in P
, by a constant
d
dependent only on the bounds on geometry of
M
, but not on
r_{1}
. Therefore, each
A_{p}
is independent of all but
d
events
A_{q},q\in P.
By Lemma
4.14
,
\mathbb{P}(A_{p})
goes to zero as
\delta\to 0
. For small enough
\delta
, the assumptions of Local Lovasz Lemma (Lemma
4.13
) will be satisfied, so none of the events
A_{p}
occur with positive probability. This proves the existence of an
(20r_{1},\delta)
-uniform triangulation on
M
. Since
r_{1}\leq r_{0}/20
was arbitary, the theorem is proved.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

(1)

The probability of the events
A_{p},p\in P
goes to
0
uniformly as
\delta\to 0.

(2)

Let
g
be a non-positively curved Riemannian metric on
B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
(standard Euclidean ball around
0
) which is
(1+\delta)
-isometric to the standard flat metric. Let
Q\subset B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
be a
4
-separated
8
-net (with respect to the metric
g
). Let
\hat{Q}
be the random set obtained by replacing each
q\in Q
independently by a uniform random point
\hat{q}\in B_{(\mathbb{R}^{n},g)}(q,1)
. Let
A
be the event that
{\rm Del}(\hat{Q})
(defined identically as before) is not a triangulation on
B_{(\mathbb{R}^{n},g)}(8)
or that one of the simplices spanned by an
(n+1)
-tuple in
\hat{Q}\cap B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
has volume less than
\delta.
Then
\mathbb{P}(A)\to 0
as
r,\delta\to 0
.

Proof.

In (1), everything happens inside the ball
B_{M}(p,28r)
. Using the inverse of the exponential map and rescaling the metric we can reduce (1) to (2).
For (2), we only sketch the proof. Suppose that we have a sequence of metrics
g_{i}
on
B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
which are
(1+\delta_{i})
-isometric to the standard metric. Let
A^{i}
be the event
A
for
B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
endowed with the metric
g_{i}
. Consider any limiting distribution
{\rm Del}(\hat{Q}_{\infty})
of the collection
{\rm Del}(\hat{Q}_{i})
, which becomes a random family of affine simplices in
B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(28)
, by Lemma
4.12
. In the standard metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the event that one of the simplices spanned by an
(n+1)
-tuple in
\hat{Q}\cap B(28)
has volume zero is zero. Similarly, the event that
\hat{Q}
is not in general position (i.e. assumptions of Lemma
4.10
are not satisfied) is zero. By Lemma
4.10
,
{\rm Del}(\hat{Q}_{\infty})
is a triangulation in
B_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(8)
almost surely. A priori, it could happen that a family of Delaunay simplices converges to an affine triangulation despite not forming a triangulation along the way. However, one can show that it could only happen if the limiting triangulation contains degenerate simplices. We know that this is a zero probability event. We can therefore apply a continuity argument to show that the probability of
A_{i}
goes to
0
as
\delta_{i}\to 0
.
∎

We record the following corollary of Theorem
4.8
.

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
X
be a symmetric space of non-compact type and let
\varepsilon>0.
There exists
\delta>0
such that for any small enough
r
, any compact locally symmetric space
\Gamma\backslash X
with
\injrad(\Gamma\backslash X)>\varepsilon
admits an
(r,\delta)
-uniform triangulation.

5.
Hessians in symmetric spaces

We compute the Hessians of the Iwasawa coordinate
H
and the spherical functions of the form
f\circ a,
where
a
is the Cartan coordinate and
f\colon\mathfrak{a}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{2}
-function invariant under the Weyl group.

5.1.
Iwasawa coordinate
H(g)

The goal of this section is to determine the Hessian of the function
H\colon G\to\mathfrak{a},
which was defined by
g=ne^{H(g)}k,n\in N,H(g)\in\mathfrak{a},k\in K
. In this section we treat it as a function on
X=G/K
and compute the Hessian on
X
. The list of eigenvalues of Hessian of
H
was implicit in the computations of
[
CMW23
]
. We provide the computation for completeness, as we were unable to locate it in the literature.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any
x\in X
there is an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}X
such that the matrix of
\Hess H
at
x
is a diagonal
\mathfrak{a}
-valued matrix with diagonal entries
0
repeated
\rk G
times and
-H_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},
where
H_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{a}
is the vector satisfying
\langle H_{\alpha},Y\rangle=\alpha(Y),Y\in\mathfrak{a}.

Recall that we identify
T_{K}X=\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{s}
via the map
Y+\mathfrak{k}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(Y-\Theta Y)
. For the remainder of this section we fix an orthonormal basis
H_{1},\ldots,H_{\rk G}
of
\mathfrak{a}
and for each
\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
we choose a vector
E_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathfrak{n}
such that the set
\{\frac{1}{2}(E_{\alpha}-\Theta E_{\alpha})\,|\,\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}\}
is an orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of
\mathfrak{a}
in
\mathfrak{s}
.

Consider the map
\psi\colon NA\to X
given by
\psi(na)=naK\in X.
This map is a surjective diffeomorphism. We will use
\psi
to transport the vector fields on
NA
to vector fields on
X
. We adopt the following notational convention. Whenever
Y\in\mathfrak{n}+\mathfrak{a}
, we will write
\mathbf{Y}^{\circ}
for the unique
NA
left invariant vector field on
NA
with
\mathbf{Y}^{\circ}(1)=Y,
and
\mathbf{Y}:=d\psi(\mathbf{Y}^{\circ})
. We note that if
[Y,Z]=W
for some
Y,Z,W\in\mathfrak{a}+\mathfrak{n}
then
[\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}]=\mathbf{W}.

Lemma 5.2
.

The fields
\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}
form an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}X
at every
x\in X
.

Proof.

NA
acts transitively and isometrically on
X
, so we only need to verify the lemma at the point
K
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}(K)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(E_{\alpha}-\Theta E_{\alpha})\in\mathfrak{s},

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}_{i}(K)=

\displaystyle H_{i}\in\mathfrak{s}.

The Lemma follows from the conditions imposed on the vectors
H_{i},E_{\alpha}
.
∎

The vector fields defined above will turn out to be the orthonormal eigenbasis of
\Hess H
at every point of
X
. Since
H
is an
\mathfrak{a}
valued function, the gradient of
H
is an element of
C^{\infty}(TX\otimes\mathfrak{a})
defined by the condition

\langle\grad H,Y\rangle=YH\in C^{\infty}(X)\otimes\mathfrak{a},
for every smooth vector field
Y\in C^{\infty}(TX).

Lemma 5.3
.

\grad H=\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\mathbf{H}_{i}\otimes H_{i}
and
dH=\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\mathbf{H}_{i}^{*}\otimes H_{i}
where
\mathbf{H}_{i}^{*}(Y):=\langle H_{i},Y\rangle.

Proof.

The two formulas are equivalent so we will just prove the second one. Note that the function
H
restricted to
A\subset NA
is the inverse of the exponential map, so the derivative restricted to
\mathfrak{a}
is the identity map, i.e.
dH(\mathbf{H}_{i})=H_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,\rk G.
As
H
is left
N
invariant we immediately get that
dH(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})=0
. This proves the lemma because
\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}
form a basis of
TX
at every point.
∎

We are now ready to compute
\Hess H\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}X\otimes T^{*}X\otimes\mathfrak{a}).

Lemma 5.4
.

\Hess H
is diagonalized in the basis
\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}
at each point of
X
. We have

\displaystyle\Hess H(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{H}_{j})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G,

\displaystyle\Hess H(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\rk G,\,\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess H(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta})=-\delta_{\alpha,\beta}H_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha,\beta\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}.

Proof.

Let
Y,Z
be vector fields on
X
. Using Koszul’s identity for the Levi-Civita connection on
X
and Lemma
5.3
, we get

\displaystyle\langle\Hess H(Y,Z),H_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle\Hess(\langle H,H_{i}\rangle)(Y,Z)=\langle\nabla_{Y}(\grad\langle H,H_{i}\rangle),Z\rangle=\langle\nabla_{Y}\mathbf{H}_{i},Z\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(Y\langle\mathbf{H}_{i},Z\rangle+\mathbf{H}_{i}\langle Y,Z\rangle-Z\langle Y,\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\langle[Y,\mathbf{H}_{i}],Z\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{H}_{i},Z],Y\rangle-\langle[Y,Z],\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle\right).

When we apply this formula for
Y,Z\in\{\mathbf{H}_{j},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}\}
, the left
NA
-invariance of the vector fields implies that all the inner products involved in the first three terms of the right hand side are constant, hence their derivatives vanish.

\displaystyle\langle\Hess H(\mathbf{H}_{j},\mathbf{H}_{k}),H_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\langle 0,\mathbf{H}_{k}\rangle-\langle 0,\mathbf{H}_{j}\rangle-\langle 0,\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle)=0,

\displaystyle\langle\Hess H(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{j}),H_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\langle[\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{i}],\mathbf{H}_{j}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{H}_{j}],\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{j}],\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(-\alpha(H_{i})\langle\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{j}\rangle-0+\alpha(H_{j})\langle\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle)=0,

\displaystyle\langle\Hess H(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta}),H_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\langle[\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{i}],\mathbf{E}_{\beta}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\beta}],\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta}],\mathbf{H}_{i}\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(-\alpha(H_{i})\langle\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta}\rangle-\beta(H_{i})\langle\mathbf{E}_{\beta},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha}\rangle-0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\alpha(H_{i}).

∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
f\colon\mathfrak{a}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
C^{2}
function and define
F\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
by
F(g):=f(H(g))
. Then,

\displaystyle\Hess F(gK)(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{H}_{j})=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial H_{i}\partial H_{j}}(H(gK))

\displaystyle\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G,

\displaystyle\Hess F(gK)(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})=\Hess F(gK)(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{i})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\rk G,\,\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess F(gK)(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta})=-\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H(gK))

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha,\beta\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}.

Corollary 5.6
.

For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we have

\displaystyle\Hess(\xi H)(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{H}_{j})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G,

\displaystyle\Hess(\xi H)(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\rk G,\,\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess(\xi H)(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta})=-\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\langle\alpha,\xi\rangle.

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha,\beta\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess(e^{\xi H})(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{H}_{j})=e^{\xi H}\xi(H_{i})\xi(H_{j})

\displaystyle\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G,

\displaystyle\Hess(e^{\xi H})(\mathbf{H}_{i},\mathbf{E}_{\alpha})=\Hess H(e^{\xi H})(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{H}_{i})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\rk G,\,\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess(e^{\xi H})(\mathbf{E}_{\alpha},\mathbf{E}_{\beta})=-e^{\xi H}\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\langle\alpha,\xi\rangle.

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha,\beta\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}

5.2.
Cartan coordinate
a(g)

The goal of this section is to explicitly compute the Hessian of
f\circ a
for any
W
-invariant
C^{2}
function
f
on
\mathfrak{a}
.
We start by defining several auxiliary vector fields on the open dense subset
KA^{++}K/K\subset X
. We keep the notation from the previous section. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{k}
and
i=1,\ldots,\rk G
. For any
gK=kaK,a\in A^{++}
put

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{Y}(kaK):=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(ke^{tY}aK)|_{t=0},\quad\mathbf{U}_{i}(kaK):=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(kae^{tH_{i}}K)|_{t=0}.

These fields are left
K
-inavaraint and a routine computation yields

(5.7)

[\mathbf{W}_{Y},\mathbf{W}_{Z}]=\mathbf{W}_{[Y,Z]},\quad[\mathbf{W}_{Y},\mathbf{U}_{i}]=0,\quad[\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j}]=0.

For any
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{++}
we have
\mathbf{W}_{Y}(e^{H}K)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(e^{tY}e^{H}K)|_{t=0}.
It follows that for any
k\in K

(5.8)

\displaystyle(e^{-H}k^{-1}\mathbf{W}_{Y})(K)=(e^{-H}\mathbf{W}_{Y})(K)=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(e^{-H}e^{tY}e^{H}K)|_{t=0}=\Ad(e^{H})Y+\mathfrak{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\Ad(e^{H})Y-\Theta\Ad(e^{H})Y)\in\mathfrak{s}.

For the last identity we used the identification
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{k}\ni Y+\mathfrak{k}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(Y-\Theta Y)\in\mathfrak{s}.
Similarly, for any
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{++},k\in K,

\displaystyle(e^{-H}k^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{i})(K)=(e^{-H}\mathbf{U}_{i})(K)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(e^{-H}e^{tH_{i}}e^{H}K)|_{t=0}=H_{i}.

Put
T_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{2}(E_{\alpha}+E_{-\alpha})=\frac{1}{2}(E_{\alpha}+\Theta E_{\alpha}),\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}.
It is fixed by
\Theta
, so it is an element of
\mathfrak{k}
. By (
5.8
),

(e^{-H}k^{-1}\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}})(K)=\sinh(\alpha(H))(E_{\alpha}-\Theta E_{\alpha}).

Recall that
E_{\alpha}
were chosen so that
\frac{1}{2}(E_{\alpha}-\Theta E_{\alpha}),\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
are pairwise orthogonal of length one. We derive the identities

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}},\mathbf{W}_{T_{\beta}}\rangle(ke^{H})=

\displaystyle 4\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\sinh^{2}(\alpha(H)),

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}},\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle(ke^{H})=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\langle[\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}},\mathbf{W}_{T_{\beta}}],\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle(ke^{H})=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j}\rangle(ke^{H})=

\displaystyle\delta_{ij},

for
\alpha,\beta\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}
and
i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G
. Finally, put
\mathbf{V}_{\alpha}:=\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}}/\|\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}}\|
. We can now state the main result of this section.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
F(gK):=f(a(g)).
For any
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{++},k\in K

\displaystyle\Hess F(ke^{H}K)(\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j})=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial H_{i}\partial H_{j}}(H)

\displaystyle\text{ for }i,j=1,\ldots,\rk G,

\displaystyle\Hess F(ke^{H}K)(\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{V}_{\alpha})=0

\displaystyle\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,\rk G,\,\alpha\in\Delta^{+},

\displaystyle\Hess F(ke^{H}K)(\mathbf{V}_{\alpha},\mathbf{V}_{\beta})=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))}\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H)

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha,\beta\in\Delta^{+}.

Proof.

We compute the gradient of
F(gK):=f(a(g)).
Let
F_{i}(ke^{H}):=\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{i}}(H)
.

\displaystyle dF(ke^{H})(\mathbf{W}_{Y})

\displaystyle=dF(e^{H})(\mathbf{W}_{Y})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(a(e^{tY}e^{H}))=0,

\displaystyle dF(ke^{H})(\mathbf{U}_{i})

\displaystyle=dF(e^{H})(\mathbf{U}_{i})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}f(a(e^{H+tH_{i}}))=\frac{\partial}{\partial H_{i}}f(H)=F_{i}(H).

Hence,

(5.10)

\grad F(ke^{H})=\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{i}}(H)\mathbf{U}_{i}(ke^{H})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\right)(ke^{H}).

We use Koszul’s formula to evaluate the Hessian of
F.

\displaystyle\Hess F(K)(\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z})=

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{Y}}\grad F,\mathbf{Z}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{Y}\langle F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{Z}\rangle+F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\rangle-\mathbf{Z}\langle\mathbf{Y},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\langle[\mathbf{Y},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}],\mathbf{Z}\rangle-\langle[F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{Z}],\mathbf{Y}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}],F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle\right)

We now substitute
\{\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}\}\subseteq\{\mathbf{W}_{T_{\alpha}},\mathbf{U}_{j}\},\alpha\in\Delta^{+},j=1,\ldots,\rk G.
Write
F_{i,j}(ke^{H}):=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial H_{i}\partial H_{j}}(H).

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{U}_{l})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{U}_{j}\langle F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{l}\rangle+F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\langle\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{U}_{l}\rangle-\mathbf{U}_{l}\langle\mathbf{U}_{j},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\langle[\mathbf{U}_{j},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}],\mathbf{U}_{l}\rangle-\langle[F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{l}],\mathbf{U}_{j}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{U}_{l}],F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}\left(F_{i,j}\langle\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{l}\rangle-F_{i,l}\langle\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j}\rangle+F_{i,j}\langle\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{l}\rangle+F_{i,l}\langle\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{j,l}.

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{U}_{j}\langle F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\rangle+F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\langle\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\rangle-\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\langle\mathbf{U}_{j},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\langle[\mathbf{U}_{j},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}],\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle[F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}],\mathbf{U}_{j}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{U}_{j},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}],F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},\mathbf{W}_{\beta})(ke^{H})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\langle F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\rangle+F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\langle\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\rangle-\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\langle\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\langle[\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}],\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\rangle-\langle[F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{W}_{\beta}],\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\rangle-\langle[\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},\mathbf{W}_{\beta}],F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}\frac{1}{2}F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}\langle\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\rangle=2\delta_{\alpha\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}F_{i}\mathbf{U}_{i}(\sinh(\alpha(H))^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\delta_{\alpha\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{\rk G}F_{i}(ke^{H})\alpha(H_{i})\sinh(\alpha(H))\cosh(\alpha(H))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\delta_{\alpha,\beta}\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H)\sinh(\alpha(H))\cosh(\alpha(H)).

We deduce that

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{U}_{j})(ke^{H}K)=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial H_{i}\partial H_{j}}(H),

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{V}_{\alpha})(ke^{H}K)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\|}\Hess F(\mathbf{U}_{i},\mathbf{W}_{\alpha})(ke^{H})=0,

\displaystyle\Hess F(\mathbf{V}_{\alpha},\mathbf{V}_{\beta})(ke^{H}K)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}\|\|\mathbf{W}_{\beta}\|}\Hess F(\mathbf{W}_{\alpha},\mathbf{W}_{\beta})(ke^{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H)\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))}.

∎

Corollary 5.11
.

The eigenvalues of
\Hess F(ke^{H})
are

\left\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\rk G},\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H)\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))},\alpha\in\tilde{\Phi}^{+}\right\}

where
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{\rk G}
are the eigenvalues of
\Hess f(H).
In case
H
is singular, and
\alpha(H)=0
, the value
\frac{\partial f}{\partial H_{\alpha}}(H)\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))}
should be replaced by
\frac{2}{\|\alpha\|^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{(\partial H_{\alpha})^{2}}(H).

Proof.

If
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{++}
, the corollary follows directly from Proposition
5.9
. To handle the singular cases we use the fact that
F
is
C^{2}
and take the limit as
\alpha(H)\to 0.

∎

5.3.
Plurisuperharmonic Functions

In this section we define, for
\Lambda
an infinite covolume discrete subgroup of
G
,
\Lambda
-equivariant functions on
X
whose gradient flows contract volume in low codimensions. These flows were recently constructed by Connell-McReynolds-Wang, who called them natural flows
[
CMW23
]
. It would be possible to use their work as a black box, but for the sake of completeness we give the full construction here. These flows will be crucial in the proof of the topological waist inequalities (Theorems
1.6
,
1.8
, and
1.11
).

Definition 5.12
.

Let
G
be a semisimple real Lie group and let
\Lambda\subset G
be a discrete subgroup. First assume that
G
has rank one. The critical exponent
\delta(\Lambda)
is defined as the exponent of convergence of the Poincare series

P_{\Lambda}(s):=\sum_{g\in\Lambda}e^{-s\alpha(a(g))},

where
a(g)
is the central Cartan coordinate and
\alpha
is the simple root of
G
.

The invariant
\delta(\Lambda)
measures the rate of growth of
\Lambda
inside
G
. Lattices in
G
have always the same critical exponent, equal to
\delta(G):=m_{\alpha}+2m_{2\alpha}
. This is the maximal critical exponent achieved by any discrete subgroup of
G
. For
G=\Sp(n,1),F_{4}^{(-20)}
the maximal critical exponent is achieved only by lattices and there is a gap between
\delta(G)
and the critical exponents of infinite covolume discrete subgroups. This was proved by Corlette
[
Cor90
]
, using quantitative property (T) for
\Sp(n,1)
and
F_{4}^{(-20)}
proved by Kostant
[
Kos69
]
.

Theorem 5.13

(
[
Cor90
, Thm 4.4]
)
.

(1)

Suppose
\Lambda
is a discrete subgroup of
G=\Sp(n,1)
. Then either
\delta(\Lambda)\leq 4n
or
\Lambda
is a lattice and
\delta(\Lambda)=4n+2
.

(2)

Suppose
\Lambda
is a discrete subgroup of
G=F_{4}^{(-20)}
. Then either
\delta(\Lambda)\leq 16
or
\Lambda
is a lattice and
\delta(\Lambda)=22
.

In higher rank groups the analogue of critical exponent is the
growth indicator function
defined by Quint
[
Qui02a
]
.

Definition 5.14
.

For any open cone
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathfrak{a}^{+}
let
\tau_{\mathcal{C}}
be the exponent of convergence of the series

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\in\Lambda\\
a(g)\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}e^{-s\|a(g)\|}.

The value of the growth indicator function
\Psi_{\Lambda}\colon\mathfrak{a}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
at a point
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
is defined as

\psi_{\Lambda}(H):=\|H\|\inf_{\mathcal{C}\ni H}\tau_{\mathcal{C}}.

The infimum here is taken over all open cones
\mathcal{C}
containing
H
.
The growth indicator function is homogeneous, that is,
\psi(tH)=t\psi(H)
for any
t>0
and concave upper semi-continuous
[
Qui02a
]
. It is non-negative on the
limit cone

\mathcal{L}

[
Ben97
]
. Moreover, if
\Lambda
is Zariski dense, then the limit cone
\mathcal{L}
has non-empty interior
[
Ben97
]
. In the rank one cases, the critical exponent and the growth indicator function are related by the identity

(5.15)

\psi_{\Lambda}=\delta(\Lambda)\alpha,

where
\alpha
is the simple root.
We say that an element
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
is
tangent to
the growth indicator function if
\xi\geq\psi_{\Lambda}
on
\mathfrak{a}^{+}
and there exists an
H\in\mathfrak{a}^{++}
with
\psi_{\Lambda}(H)=\xi(H).

Let
\mathcal{F}:=G/P=K/M
be the Furstenberg boundary of
G
, and let
\Lambda<G
be a discrete subgroup. At this point we need to remind that our convention with
H
is different from
[
Qui02b
]
or
[
EO23
]
. If we define
\tilde{H}(g)
by
g=ke^{\tilde{H}(g)}n
, then the Iwasawa coordinate of
[
Qui02b
,
EO23
]
is
\tilde{H}
. To obtain the formulas in terms of “our"
H
one needs to use the formula
H(g)=-\tilde{H}(g^{-1}).

Definition 5.16
.

Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
. A probability measure
\nu
on
\mathcal{F}
is
(\Lambda,\xi)
-conformal if

\frac{d\gamma_{*}\nu}{d\nu}(kP)=e^{\xi(H(k^{-1}\gamma))},

for all
\gamma\in\Lambda.

Here
\gamma_{*}\nu(B)=\nu(\gamma^{-1}(B))
for any Borel subset
B\subset\mathcal{F}
. We will need the following result from Quint
[
Qui02b
]
.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
\Lambda
be a discrete subgroup of
\Isom(X)
. Then if
\xi
is tangent to
\psi_{\Lambda}
, there exists a
(\Lambda,\xi)
-conformal measure.

We have to recall one more result due to Quint and Lee-Oh. We say that a subset
\Omega\in\Phi^{+}
is strongly orthogonal if for every
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega
we have
\pm\alpha\pm\beta\not\in\Omega.

Theorem 5.18

(
[
LO24
, Thm 7.1]
,
[
Oh02
]
)
.

Let
G
be semisimple Lie group with no rank one factors. Let
\Theta
be the half sum of the roots in a strongly orthogonal subset of
\Phi^{+}
. Then, for any discrete
\Lambda\subset G
which is not a lattice

\psi_{\Lambda}\leq 2\rho-\Theta.

For
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
and
\nu
a
(\Lambda,\xi)
-invariant conformal measure the function
\EO_{\nu}
associated to
\nu
is defined by the formula

(5.19)

\EO_{\nu}(g):=\int_{K}e^{\xi(H(k^{-1}g))}d\nu(kP)

for
g\in G
(see
[
EO23
, Section 3]
.) Since
\EO_{\nu}
is left
\Lambda
-invariant and right
K
-invariant, it descends to a function on
\Lambda\backslash X
which by abuse of notation we also denote by
\EO_{\nu}
. It is the density of the Burger-Roblin measure projected from
\Lambda\backslash G
to
\Lambda\backslash X
(see
[
EO23
, Lemma 9.1]
). One can check that
\EO_{\nu}
is an eigenfunction for the Laplacian (
[
EO23
, Lemma 3.4]
). We will be interested in determining ranges of dimensions
k
for which the gradient of
\EO_{\nu}
contracts the
k
-dimensional volume.

To this end, for
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
, we define
\tau_{k}(\xi)
to be the sum of the k-largest elements (possibly with multiplicity) of the multiset

(5.20)

\{||\xi||^{2},0,..,0\}\cup\{-\langle\alpha,\xi\rangle|\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\},

where the number of 0’s in the first set in the union is
\text{rank}(G)-1
. We will see in a moment that this multiset is exactly the eigenvalues of the Hessian of
g\mapsto e^{\xi(H(k^{-1}g))}
. Let
\Omega_{d}=\{\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}|\tau_{\text{dim}(X)-d}(\xi)<0\}
. We do not undertake here a close study of the sets
\Omega_{d}
. The following lemma records a few basic properties and will be more than sufficient for our purposes.

Lemma 5.21
.

\Omega_{d}
is a convex open subset of
\mathfrak{a}^{*}
. Moreover,
\Omega_{d}\cup\{0\}
is star-shaped with respect to the origin
0
.

Proof.

That
\Omega_{d}
is open and that
\Omega_{d}\cup\{0\}
is star-shaped with respect to
0
is clear. We only need to check that
\Omega_{d}
is convex. That
\Omega_{d}
is convex follows from the fact that

||\lambda\xi_{1}+(1-\lambda)\xi_{2}||^{2}\leq\max(||\xi_{1}||^{2},||\xi_{2}||^{2}).

and

-\langle\alpha,\lambda\xi_{1}+(1-\lambda)\xi_{2}\rangle\leq\max(-\langle\alpha,\xi_{1}\rangle,-\langle\alpha,\xi_{2}\rangle)

for
\lambda\in(0,1)
.
∎

The
k–trace

\tr_{k}(Q)
of a bilinear form Q is the sum of the k largest eigenvalues of Q. The
k
-Jacobian
{\rm Jac}_{k}(T)
of a linear map
T:V\rightarrow W
between inner product spaces is the largest value, over all
k
-dimensional subspaces
V_{0}
of
V
for the absolute value of the Jacobian of the map
T|_{V_{0}}:V_{0}\rightarrow T(V_{0})
. We will use the following result from
[
CMW23
]
, which says that flowing along the gradient of a function whose Hessian has negative
k
-traces exponentially decreases the volume of any k-dimensional submanifold.

Theorem 5.22
.

[
CMW23
, Theorem 1.1]

Let
\phi\colon M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be any
C^{2}
–smooth function
whose associated
C^{1}
–smooth vector field
\grad\phi
is complete. Let
\Phi_{t}:M\rightarrow M
be the associated one-parameter subgroup of self-diffeomorphisms that the vector field integrates to. If
k
is a positive integer and

(5.23)

\tr_{k}(\Hess(\phi))\leq-\varepsilon

for all
x\in M
, then

{\rm Jac}_{k}(\Phi_{t}(x))\leq e^{-\varepsilon t}

for all
t>0
and
x\in M
. In particular, if
\epsilon>0
then
\Phi_{t}
uniformly exponentially contracts the k–volume
as t increases.

We note that the fact that these flows decrease the volumes of
k
-submaniflolds exponentially fast is also a consequence of the first variation formula for gradient flows (
3.10
) and Grönewall’s inequality.
We call a function that satisfies (
5.23
)
(k,\epsilon)
-plurisuperharmonic
(
(k,\epsilon)
-PSpH) or
k
-plurisuperharmonic (
k
-PSpH) if we just require the negativity of
k
-traces. The set of
k
-plurisuperharmonic functions is a convex cone. On general Riemannian manifolds the related
k
-plurisubharmonic functions (which are minus
k
-plurisuperharmonic) were introduced and studied by Harvey and Lawson Jr (see
[
HLJ12
]
).

Proposition 5.24
.

Suppose that
\nu
is a
(\Lambda,\xi)
-conformal measure on
\mathcal{F}=G/P
with
\tau_{k}(\xi)<0.
Then the function
\EO_{\nu}
is
k
-PSpH.

Proof.

Note that for any fixed
f\in G
the eigenvalues for the Hessian of
gK\mapsto e^{\xi(H(f^{-1}g))}
are given by the list (
5.20
) by the computations in Section
5.1
and Corollary
5.6
(see the proofs of Propositions
5.26
and
5.27
further down for more details on the octonionic hyperbolic and
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{R})
cases). Then the statement follows from the fact that
\EO_{\nu}
is obtained by averaging functions of this form.

∎

Definition 5.25
.

For rank one symmetric spaces
X
, we define the quantity
C_{X}(d)
to be the supremal
\delta>0
so the function
E_{\nu}
for every discrete subgroup
\Lambda
of
\Isom(X)
with critical exponent smaller than
\delta
is
(n-d)
-PSpH. It is straightforward to get a lower bound for
C_{X}(d)
by computing the Hessians of exponentials of negative multiples of Busemann functions for
X
as in the proof of Proposition
5.26
below. See Table
3
for explicit estimates. For the real hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{R}}
one can check that
C_{X}(d)=n-d-1
, which matches the exponent of the growth for the volumes of balls in
(n-d)
-dimensional hyperbolic space (with the constant curvature
-1
metric).

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{R}}}(d)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}n-1-d&d=0,\ldots,n-1\\
0&d=n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}}}(d)\geq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}2n-2d&d=0,1\\
2n-1-d&d=2,\ldots,2n-1\\
0&d=2n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{H}}}(d)\geq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}4n+2-2d&d=0,1,2,3\\
4n-1-d&d=4,\ldots,4n-1\\
0&d=4n,\end{cases}

\displaystyle C_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathbb{O}}}(d)\geq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}22-2d&d=0,1,\ldots,7\\
15-d&d=8,\ldots,15\\
0&d=16,\end{cases}

Table 3.
C_{X}
for rank one spaces.

5.4.
Dimension gap
r(X)

Let
X
be a symmetric space. The quantity
r(X)
is defined to be the greatest
d
so that every infinite co-volume quotient
\Gamma\backslash X
admits an
(n-d)
-PSpH function. If
\Lambda
has torsion we ask for a
\Lambda
-invariant
k
-PSpH function on
X
, to avoid dealing with technicalities at the singular points of the orbifold
\Lambda\backslash X
). In this section, we obtain lower bounds for
r(X)
in the octonionic hyperbolic and
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{R})
cases. Similar computation could be carried for classical groups like
\SO(p,q)
, but we limit ourselves to those two cases in the interest of space.

Proposition 5.26
.

r(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathbb{O}})\geq 2.

Proof.

Recall that if
\alpha
is the simple root of
\rm{F}_{4}^{(-20)}
, then the positive roots are
\{\alpha,2\alpha\}
and the multiplicities are
m_{\alpha}=8
and
m_{2\alpha}=7
.
By Theorem
5.13
, we know that the critical exponent
\delta(\Lambda)
of an infinite covolume discrete subgroup
\Lambda
of
G=F_{4}^{(-20)}
is at most 16.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, define
F:X=G/K\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

F_{s}(gK):=e^{s\alpha(H(g))}

where recall
g=ne^{H(g)}k
. Then relative to the orthonormal basis from Section
5.1
, it follows from Corollary
5.6
that
\Hess(F_{s})/(sF_{s})
is equal to the diagonal matrix

\begin{bmatrix}s&&&&&&\\
&-1&&&&&\\
&&\ddots&&&&\\
&&&-1&&&\\
&&&&-2&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&&&-2\\
\end{bmatrix},

where there are eight -1’s and seven -2’s on the diagonal. For
s\leq 16
, one can check that the
k
-trace is negative for
k=16,15,14
. We now let
\nu
be a
(\delta(\Lambda),\Lambda)
-conformal density, which exists by Theorem
5.17
. The function
\EO_{\nu}
(
5.19
) is obtained by averaging translates of
F
, so it is
(n-d)
-PSpH, for
d=0,1,2.
This shows that
r(\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathbb{O}})\geq 2
.

∎

Proposition 5.27
.

r(\SL_{n}(\mathbb{R})/\SO(n))\geq\lfloor n/8\rfloor-1
.

Proof.

Denote the positive roots of
\SL_{n}(\mathbb{R})
by
\Phi^{+}=\{\alpha_{i,j}|1\leq i<j\leq n\}
and the simple roots by
\Delta^{+}=\{\alpha_{i,i+1}|i=1,..,n-1\}
. Here
\alpha_{i,j}
evaluated at an element

\begin{bmatrix}e_{1}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&e_{n}\\
\end{bmatrix}

of
\mathfrak{a}
is equal to
e_{i}-e_{j}
. For
\xi\in\mathfrak{a}^{*}
a nonnegative linear combination of simple roots, define
F\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

F(gK):=e^{\xi(H(g))}

Then relative to the orthonormal basis from Section
5.1
, by Corollary
5.6

\Hess(F)/F
is equal to the diagonal matrix

\begin{bmatrix}||\xi||^{2}&&&&&&\\
&0&&&&&\\
&&\ddots&&&&\\
&&&0&&&\\
&&&&-\langle\alpha_{1},\xi\rangle&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&&&-\langle\alpha_{|\Phi^{+}|},\xi\rangle\\
\end{bmatrix}.

Here there are
n-2
upper-left diagonal entries equal to zero, and the
\alpha_{1},..,\alpha_{|\Phi^{+}|}
are the elements of
\Phi^{+}
.

Recall that
\rho
is the half sum of the multiset of positive roots, and
\Theta
is the half sum of the roots in a maximal strongly orthogonal subset of
\Phi^{+}
, which we choose to be
\{\alpha_{1,n},\alpha_{2,n-1},\ldots\}
. By Theorem
5.18
, the growth indicator function
\psi_{\Lambda}
of every infinite covolume discrete subgroup
\Lambda\subset\Isom(X)
satisfies
\psi_{\Lambda}\leq 2\rho-\Theta
. By Proposition
5.24
, the formula (
5.19
) for the functions
E_{\nu}
, and the definition of
\Omega_{d}
(see (
5.20
)), it is therefore enough to check that
\xi=2\rho-\Theta
is in
\Omega_{d}
for
d\leq\lfloor n/8\rfloor-1
.

In our case, we have that

2\Theta=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\alpha_{i,n-i}=e_{1}+..+e_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}-e_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1}-..-e_{n}

and

2\rho=(n-1)e_{1}+(n-3)e_{2}+...+(1-n)e_{n}.

We give the computation in the case when
n
is even; the computation in the case when
n
is odd, which we omit, is similar and yields the same lower bound. Let
n=2m
. We have

\displaystyle||2\rho-\Theta||^{2}

\displaystyle=2((n-3/2)^{2}+(n-7/2)^{2}+..+(1/2)^{2})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{1}{4}(1+4k)^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(m+8\left(\frac{m(m-1)}{2}\right)+16\frac{(m-1)m(2m-1)}{6}\right).

We also have

\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle=2(j-i)-\mathbb{I}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<\lfloor n/2\rfloor,\\
j>\lfloor n/2\rfloor\end{subarray}}(i,j)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{j>i}\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2\rho,2\rho-\Theta\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2\rho,2\rho\rangle-\langle 2\rho,\Theta\rangle

\displaystyle=2(1^{2}+3^{2}+..+(n-1)^{2})-2(\frac{1}{2}+..+\frac{n-3}{2}+\frac{n-1}{2}))

\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{m(2m-1)(2m+1)}{3}\right)-m^{2}.

The trace of
\Hess(F)/F
is then equal to

\displaystyle||2\rho-\Theta||^{2}-\sum_{j>i}\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{m}{2}+2m(m-1)+\frac{4(m-1)m(2m-1)}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{2m(2m-1)(2m+1)}{3}-m^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2m^{2}-\frac{3}{2}m+\frac{4}{3}(2m^{3}-3m^{2}+m)-\frac{2}{3}(4m^{3}-m)+m^{2}

\displaystyle=-(2-4+1)m^{2}+(10/3-3/2)m=-m^{2}+\frac{11}{6}m.

We now need to check what is the largest
d
is so that if we omit the
d
largest possible values of
\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle

from
\sum_{j>i}\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle
we still get a negative value when we subtract it from
||2\rho-\Theta||^{2}
. It will then be the case that the trace of
\Hess(F)/F
through every
(n-d)
-dimensional tangent plane is negative.

The largest possible value of
\langle\alpha_{i,j},2\rho-\Theta\rangle
is
2(n-1)-1=2n-3=4m-3
. We can thus omit at least

\left\lfloor\frac{m^{2}-\frac{11}{6}m}{4m-3}\right\rfloor\geq\left\lfloor\frac{m^{2}-\frac{11}{6}m}{4m}\right\rfloor\geq\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4}m-\frac{11}{24}\right\rfloor=\left\lfloor\frac{1}{8}n-\frac{11}{24}\right\rfloor\geq\left\lfloor\frac{1}{8}n\right\rfloor-1

terms from the sum and still obtain a negative value.

∎

6.
Monotonicity estimates in rank one symmetric spaces

In Section
3.1
, we have fixed a smooth non-increasing function
\chi\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
\chi(t)=1
for
t<0
,
\chi(t)=0
for
t>1
and
\chi^{\prime}(t)\geq-2
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We have also defined
\chi_{r}(t):=\chi(t-r).
We keep this choice and notation fixed in the remainder of the paper.

Let
G
be a rank one simple real Lie group. We keep the notations from Section
3.2
and
3.3
.
Let
\kappa\colon\{1,\ldots,\dim X-1\}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, be given as

X

\dim X

\kappa(k)=k-1

\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

n

\kappa(k)=\begin{cases}k-1\\
2n\end{cases}

\begin{array}[]{l}k=1,\ldots,2n-1\\
k=2n-1\end{array}

\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{n}

2n

\kappa(k)=\begin{cases}k-1\\
4n-4+2i\end{cases}

\begin{array}[]{l}k=1,\ldots,4n-3\\
k=4n-3+i\end{array}

\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{H}}^{n}

4n

\kappa(k)=\begin{cases}k-1\\
8+2i\end{cases}

\begin{array}[]{l}k=1,\ldots,9\\
k=9+i\end{array}

\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{2}

16

Table 4.
\kappa
in rank one groups.

We define a spherical function
\ff\colon G\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

(6.1)

\ff(g):=\frac{1}{2\|\alpha\|}\log(2\cosh(2\alpha(a(g)))),

where
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
is the simple root. This function is a smooth approximation to the distance to the origin
K\in X
, which is given by

d(gK,K)=\|a(g)\|=\frac{\alpha(a(g))}{\|\alpha\|}.

Lemma 6.2
.

For any
x\in X
, any
k
-dimensional subspace
W\subset T_{x}X,
we have

\tr\Hess\ff|_{W}\geq\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|.

Proof.

Let
H_{1}\in\mathfrak{a}^{+}
be the element with
\|H_{1}\|=1.
By Proposition
5.9
, the eigenvalues of
\Hess\ff
at a point
ke^{H}K\in X,k\in K,H=tH_{1}\in\mathfrak{a}^{++}
are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial H_{1})^{2}}\frac{1}{2\|\alpha\|}\log(2\cosh(2\alpha(H)))=

\displaystyle\frac{\|\alpha\|}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial t)^{2}}\log\cosh(2t)=\|\alpha\|\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\frac{\sinh(2t)}{\cosh(2t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\|\alpha\|(1-\tanh^{2}(2t))>0,\text{ with multiplicity }1,

\displaystyle\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))}\frac{\partial}{\partial H_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{2}\log\cosh(2\alpha(H))=

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|\frac{\cosh(\alpha(H))\sinh(2\alpha(H))}{\sinh(\alpha(H))\cosh(2\alpha(H))}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\alpha\|\text{ with multiplicity }m_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\frac{\cosh(2\alpha(H))}{\sinh(2\alpha(H))}\frac{\partial}{\partial H_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{2}\log\cosh(2\alpha(H))=

\displaystyle 2\|\alpha\|\text{ with multiplicity }m_{2\alpha}.

The trace
\tr\Hess\ff(x)|_{W}
is bounded from below by the sum of bottom
k
eigenvalues of
\Hess\ff(x)
. Therefore, for
k=1
the lower bound is
0
, for
k=1+i,i\leq m_{\alpha}
, the lower bound is
i\|\alpha\|
and for
k=1+m_{\alpha}+i,i<m_{2\alpha}
, the lower bound is
(1+m_{\alpha}+2i)\|\alpha\|
. Analyzing these bounds case by case, we arrive at the lower bound
\tr\Hess\ff(x)|_{W}\geq\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|
.
∎

Let
S\subset X
be a varifold, let
gK\in G/K=X
and let
r\geq 0
. Put

(6.3)

v_{S}(gK,r):=\int_{S}\chi_{r}(\ff(s^{-1}g))d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)=\int_{S}\chi_{r}(\ff(g^{-1}s))d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK).

The value
v_{S}(gK,r)
roughly corresponds to the mass of
S
in the
r
-ball around
gK
. Because of the smoothing effect of
\chi
it is much more convenient to work with than
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S\cap B(gK,r)),
which is the actual mass in the
r
-ball.

Lemma 6.4
.

(1)

Let
S\subset X
be a stationary
k
-varifold. For any
x\in X
,
r>s>0

v_{S}(gK,r)\geq e^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|(r-s)}v_{S}(gK,s).

(2)

There is
\delta>0
such that
v_{S}(x,0)\geq\delta
for any stationary
k
-varifold
S\subset X
and
\mathcal{H}^{k}
-almost every point of
S
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows from Lemma
3.11
applied to function
\mathfrak{f}.
Part (2) follows from Lemma
3.12
and our choice of
\chi.

∎

In
[
And82
, Theorem 1]
Anderson proved that on any complete simply connected negatively curved manifold
N
of sectional curvature at most
-a
the volume of the intersection of
B(x,r)\cap S,
where is
S
is a stationary
k
-varifolds and
x
is a regular point of
S
, is at least equal to the volume of an
r
-ball in the
k
-dimensional contractible space with constant sectional curvature
-a.
Up to a multiplicative constant, our estimate is comparable with Anderson’s for
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{R}}
but it is strictly stronger for
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\mathbb{H}},\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\mathbb{O}}
for
k>2n-1,4n-3,9
respectively. This seemingly modest improvement is at the core of our arguments in Sections
7
and
8
.

7.
Stationary Varifolds and matrix coefficients

Let
\Gamma
be a discrete subgroup of
G
. In this section we will use the monotonicity estimates to control the decay of certain matrix coefficients of the right regular representation
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
. The discussion applies to all discrete subgroups of
G
although the corollaries seem to be most interesting in the case of lattices. We use the tension between the monotonicity estimates and the decay of matrix coefficients to prove Theorem
1.14
.

7.1.
Convolutions, unitary representations and spherical functions.

The discussion in this part applies to all semi-simple real Lie groups with no restrictions on the rank. Let us recall the definition of the convolution for functions on
C(G)
. For
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in C_{c}(G)
we put

\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2}(h)=\int_{G}\varphi_{2}(g^{-1}h)\varphi_{1}(g)dg=\int_{G}\varphi_{1}(hg^{-1})\varphi_{2}(g)dg.

The same definition applies when only one of the functions is in
C_{c}(G)
and the other is only locally
L^{1}
-integrable or when both functions are
L^{2}
-integrable. In particular, if
f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
, then
f\ast\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
for any
\varphi\in C_{c}(G).
Let us explain the interplay between convolutions and right regular unitary representations in more detail.
Let
\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}\colon G\to\mathscr{U}(L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G))
be the right regular representation

\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g)f(h):=f(hg),\quad f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G).

Note that
\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}
is a left action
\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g_{1})\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g_{2})f=\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g_{1}g_{2})f.

For any
\varphi\in L^{1}(G)
we define

[\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi)f](h):=\int_{G}\varphi(g)(\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g)f(h))dg,\quad f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G),h\in G.

The resulting function
\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi)f
is left
\Gamma
-invariant and descends to a square integrable function on
\Gamma\backslash G.

We have

(7.1)

\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi)f(h)=\int_{G}\varphi(g)f(hg)dg=f\ast\check{\varphi}(h),\text{ where }\check{\varphi}(g):=\varphi(g^{-1}).

Convolutions play nicely with the operators defined just above

(7.2)

\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi_{1})\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi_{2})f=\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2})f,\quad\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in C_{c}(G).

In particular
\varphi\mapsto\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\varphi)
is an algebra homomorphism from the
C_{c}(G)
with convolution to the algebra of bounded operators on
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G).
We also have

(7.3)

(f\ast\varphi_{1})\ast\varphi_{2}=f\ast(\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2}),\quad f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G),\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in C_{c}(G).

and

(7.4)

\langle f_{1}\ast\varphi,f_{2}\rangle=\langle f_{1},f_{2}\ast\check{\varphi}\rangle.

Recall that
K
was a fixed maximal compact subgroup of
G
. A function
f\colon G\to\mathbb{C}
is called spherical if
f(k_{1}gk_{2})=f(g)
for all
k_{1},k_{2}
. We write
L^{p}(G\sslash K),C(G\sslash K)
for the spaces of
L^{p}
-integrable and continuous spherical function respectively. In the sequel we will heavily rely on the fact that convolution of spherical functions is commutative.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in L^{1}(G\sslash K)
be spherical functions. Then
\varphi_{1}\ast\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{2}\ast\varphi_{1}.

Proof.

It follows from
[
GV88
, Prop. 1.5.2]
combined with the definition
[
GV88
, Def. 1.5.1]
.
∎

For any
k
-varifold
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
write
\tilde{S}
for the lift to
X
.

\tilde{S}=\Gamma S=\{x\in X\mid\Gamma x\in S\}.

Since being stationary is a local property,
S
is stationary if and only if
\tilde{S}
is.

Definition 7.6
.

For
\varphi
a spherical function the formula

(\tilde{S}\ast\varphi)(h):=\int_{\tilde{S}}\varphi(g^{-1}h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(gK)

defines a left
\Gamma
-invariant, right
K
-invariant function on
G
which descends to a right
K
-invariant function in
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
, denoted
S\ast\varphi
.

From now on let us fix a smooth compactly supported spherical test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
such that
\varphi(g)\geq 0,\int_{G}\varphi(g)dg=1
. The constants appearing in the results below will depend on our choice of
\varphi
but not on
S
or
\Gamma
.
We put

\mathfrak{u}:=S\ast\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)\text{ and }\Psi(g):=\langle\mathfrak{u},\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g^{-1})\mathfrak{u}\rangle=\langle\mathfrak{u},\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g)\mathfrak{u}\rangle.

Choose a Borel measurable fundamental domain
S_{0}\subset\tilde{S}
of
S
, so that
\tilde{S}=\bigsqcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma S_{0}.

Lemma 7.7
.

For any
r\geq 0
and any smooth compactly supported spherical function
\Phi\colon G\to\mathbb{R}

\int_{G}\Phi(g)\Psi(g)dg=\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}\left(\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}uh)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\right)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK).

Proof.

By the bi-linearity of the inner product we have that the left side equals

\int_{G}\Phi(g)\langle S\ast\varphi,\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g^{-1})(S\ast\varphi)\rangle dg=\langle S\ast\varphi,\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(\check{\Phi})(S\ast\varphi)\rangle=\langle S\ast\varphi,(S\ast\varphi)\ast\Phi\rangle.

Let us compute the convolution on the right side of the product. Let
h\in\Gamma\backslash G.

\displaystyle(S\ast\varphi)\ast\Phi(h)=

\displaystyle\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\varphi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\Phi(g)dg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\varphi(s^{-1}hg^{-1}))\Phi(g)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}(\varphi\ast\Phi)(s^{-1}h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}(\Phi\ast\varphi)(s^{-1}h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})\varphi(g)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dg

The second to last equality above is where we use the assumption that
\Phi
is spherical, hence
\varphi\ast\Phi=\Phi\ast\varphi
. We can now evaluate the inner product in question

\displaystyle\langle S\ast\varphi,S\ast\varphi\ast\Phi\rangle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}(S\ast\varphi)(h)\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})\varphi(g)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dgdh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\varphi(u^{-1}h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})\varphi(g)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dgdh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{S_{0}}\varphi(u^{-1}\gamma h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)\right)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})\varphi(g)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dg\right)dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}\gamma hg^{-1})\varphi(g)\varphi(u^{-1}\gamma h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dgd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)\right)dh

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}hg^{-1})\varphi(g)\varphi(u^{-1}h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dhdgd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}\int_{G}\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}uh)\varphi(g)\varphi(hg)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)dhdgd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}\left(\int_{\tilde{S}}\Phi(s^{-1}uh)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\right)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK).

To go from the third-last to the second-last line, we do change of variables on
h
first by left multiplication by
u
and then by right multiplication by
g
.

∎

7.2.
Monotonicity and the decay of matrix coefficients

From now on we restrict to the rank one case. Let
\ff
be the function defined in (
6.1
). Put

\displaystyle v_{S}(g,r):=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}\chi_{r}(\ff(s^{-1}g))d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\quad\text{ (same as before)},

\displaystyle w_{S}(g,r):=

\displaystyle\int_{G}v_{S}(gh)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dh=\int_{G}\left(\int_{\tilde{S}}\chi_{r}(\ff(s^{-1}gh))(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\right)dh.

Applying Lemma
7.7
to
\Phi(g)=\chi_{r}(\ff(g))
, we get the identity

(7.8)

\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg=\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}v_{S}(sh,r)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)=\int_{S_{0}}w_{S}(s,r)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK).

Recall
\mathfrak{u}:=S\ast\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
. Put
\mathfrak{u}_{0}:=\mathfrak{u}-\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}
. Then, since
\int_{G}\varphi(g)dg=1
, we have
\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\mathfrak{u}_{0}(g)dg=0.
Put
\mathfrak{u}_{1}:=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}.
Let

\Psi_{i}(g):=\langle\mathfrak{u}_{i},\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g^{-1})\mathfrak{u}_{i}\rangle,i=0,1.

Then
\Psi(g)=\langle\mathfrak{u},\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g^{-1})\mathfrak{u}\rangle=\Psi_{0}(g)+\Psi_{1}(g)
. It is not hard to compute
\Psi_{1}(g)=\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)},
so

(7.9)

\Psi(g)=\Psi_{0}(g)+\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}.

The function
\Psi
plays a key role in the proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
is a stationary
k
-varifold. Then

(1)

\Psi(1)\geq c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
,

(2)

\int_{G}\chi_{0}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg\geq c\Psi(1)
,

(3)

for any
r\geq 0

\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg\geq ce^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\Psi(1).

Proof.

(1)

\Psi(1)=\langle S\ast\varphi,S\ast\varphi\rangle=\int_{S_{0}}\int_{\tilde{S}}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(s^{-1}u)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK),

Let
\varepsilon>0
be such that
\varphi\ast\check{\varphi}(g)\geq\varepsilon
for all
g\in B_{G}(\varepsilon)
. By Lemma
3.12
,
\mathcal{H}^{k}(s^{-1}\tilde{S}\cap B(\varepsilon))>\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
for
\mathcal{H}^{k}
-almost all
s\in\tilde{S}
. Therefore
\Psi(1)\geq\varepsilon\delta\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).
The constants
\varepsilon,\delta
are independent of
S
and
\Gamma
.

(2)

Since
\varphi,\chi(\ff)
are both non-negative continuous and positive at
1
, there is a constant
c>0
such that
\varphi\ast\chi_{0}(\ff)\geq c\varphi.

We have

\int_{G}\chi_{0}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg=\langle S\ast\varphi,S\ast\varphi\ast\chi_{0}(\ff)\rangle\geq c\langle S\ast\varphi,S\ast\varphi\rangle=c\Psi(1).

(3)

By (
7.8
), Lemma
6.4
and point (2) proved just above

\displaystyle\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg=

\displaystyle\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}v_{S}(uhK,r)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}v_{S}(uhK,0)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\int_{G}\chi_{0}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg\geq ce^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\Psi(1).

∎

We have now enough tools to prove Theorem
1.14
. We reproduce the statement below.

Theorem
1.14
.

Let
M
be an octonionic hyperbolic
16
-manifold. There is a constant
c>0
such that any stationary
k
-varifold
S\subset M
satisfies
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)\geq c\vol(M)
for
k=14,15
.

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Any finite volume octonionic manifold is of the form
M=\Gamma\backslash X
where
\Gamma
is a lattice in
F_{4}^{(-20)}
and
X=\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{2}
. In that case
\vol(M)=\vol(\Gamma\backslash G).

By Lemma
7.10
, for any
r\geq 0

\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg\geq ce^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\Psi(1).

On the other hand

\displaystyle\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg=

\displaystyle\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi_{1}(g)dg+\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi_{0}(g)dg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))dg+\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi_{0}(g)dg.

Therefore

(7.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))dg\geq ce^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}\Psi(1)-\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi_{0}(g)dg.

After unfolding the definition of
\ff,\chi_{r}
, integrating in spherical coordinates and changing variable
t:=\alpha(H)
we get:

\displaystyle\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))dg\geq

\displaystyle c\int_{0}^{(r+1)\|\alpha\|}\sinh(t)^{m_{\alpha}}\sinh(2t)^{m_{2\alpha}}dt\geq ce^{r(m_{\alpha}+2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|}.

The function
\Psi_{0}
is a spherical matrix coefficient of the unitary representation

L^{2}_{0}(\Gamma\backslash G):=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)\mid\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}fdg=0\right\}.

The latter has no fixed vectors, so after decomposing this representation into irreducible unitary representations Lemma
3.17
yields

|\Psi_{0}(k_{1}e^{H}k_{2})|\leq ce^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\alpha(H)}(1+\|H\|)\Psi_{0}(1)\leq ce^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\alpha(H)}(1+\|H\|)\Psi(1).

Using spherical integration once again, we get

\displaystyle\left|\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi_{0}(g)dg\right|\leq

\displaystyle c\Psi(1)\int_{0}^{(r+1)\|\alpha\|}\sinh(t)^{m_{\alpha}}\sinh(2t)^{m_{2\alpha}}e^{(1-m_{2\alpha})t}(1+t)dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\Psi(1)e^{r(m_{\alpha}+m_{2\alpha}+1)\|\alpha\|}(1+r).

Plugging the previous inequalities back to (
7.11
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}e^{(m_{\alpha}+2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r}\geq

\displaystyle\Psi(1)(c_{1}e^{\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|r}-c_{2}e^{(m_{\alpha}+m_{2\alpha}+1)\|\alpha\|r}(r+1))

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\geq

\displaystyle\Psi(1)(c_{1}e^{(\kappa(k)-m_{\alpha}-2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r}-c_{2}e^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r}(r+1))

Put
A(r):=(c_{1}e^{(\kappa(k)-m_{\alpha}-2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r}-c_{2}e^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r}(r+1))
. Our conditions on
k
(i.e.
k=14,15
) are chosen exactly to guarantee that
\kappa(k)-m_{\alpha}-2m_{2\alpha}>1-m_{2\alpha}
(i.e.
\kappa(k)>1+m_{\alpha}+m_{2\alpha}=\dim X
). We choose big enough
r
so that
A(r)>0
. Then, by Lemma
7.10
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\geq

\displaystyle\Psi(1)A(r)\geq c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)A(r)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)\geq

\displaystyle cA(r)\vol(\Gamma\backslash G).

Theorem
1.14
is proved.
∎

8.
Collapsing Small Area Subsets to Lower Dimensions

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.16
. Let us recall the statement.

Theorem
1.16
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
M
be an octonionic hyperbolic
16
-manifold with injectivity radius at least
\varepsilon.
There exists an
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon)>0
with the following property. Any rectifiable
k
-varifold
S
with
k=14,15
admits a homotopy
h\colon S\times[0,1]\to M
such that

(1)

h(s,0)=s
for all
s\in S
,

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{k-1}(h(S,1)))<\infty,

(3)

\mathcal{H}^{k+1}(h(S,[0,1])\leq c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S),
with
c=c(\varepsilon).

Stated more plainly, Theorem
1.16
says that a small codimension-
2
subset of
M
can be efficiently deformed into a codimension-
3
set. The fact that we can perform an actual deformation, not just fill the cycle in homology, will be of crucial importance in Section
12
where we use it to prove a branched cover stability theorem for octonionic hyperbolic manifolds.

8.1.
Shortcut to an interesting corollary

Before heading toward the proof of Theorem
1.16
, we sketch here a simpler proof of a consequence of Theorem
1.16
: every integral cycle
F
in codimension one or two with area smaller than some constant times the volume of the ambient space can be homotoped to a lower-dimensional set. First, we can assume that
F
is connected and that
\mathcal{H}^{k}(F)\leq c\vol(M),
where
c
is the constant from Theorem
1.14
.

We consider two cases according to whether
\pi_{1}(F)
includes to a finite index subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M)
or not.

In the first case, we pass to a finite cover
M^{\prime}
such that a connected component
F^{\prime}
of the lift of
F
to
M^{\prime}
has
\pi_{1}(F^{\prime})
surjecting onto
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
. Let
F^{\prime\prime}
be an integral cycle homologous to
F^{\prime}
mod
2
, with the minimal
k
-dimensional volume. Such representatives exists by
[
Alm68
, Theorem 1.4]
and are stationary. By Theorem
1.14
, this cycle must be trivial, since there are no stationary
k
-varifolds in
M^{\prime}
of
k
-volume less than
c\vol(M^{\prime})
. We deduce that
F
is null-homologous mod
2
, and conclude by
[
Whi84
]
that it can be homotoped to sets of arbitrary low
k
-dimensional volume. Then an application of Federer-Fleming deformation (see Section
4.1
) homotopes it into a
(k-1)
-dimensional set.

In the second case, we can lift
F
to an infinite sheeted cover
\Lambda\backslash X,
where
\Lambda
is the inclusion of
\pi_{1}(F)
to
\pi_{1}(M)
. Then
[
CMW23
, Theorem 1.1]
(see also section
5.3
) implies that
F
can be homotoped to a set of arbitrarily small
k
-volume
F^{\prime}
. Applying the Federer-Fleming deformation to
F^{\prime}
(see Section
4
) and projecting down to
M
gives the desired homotopy of
F
to a lower dimensional set.

8.2.
Sketch of the argument

Let
X=\mathbb{H}_{\mathbb{O}}^{2}
and
G=F_{4}^{(-20)}
. Any compact octonionic hyperbolic manifold
M
is of the form
\Gamma\backslash X
, where
\Gamma
is a cocompact lattice in
G
.

We will construct the homotopy one step at a time, arguing that we can always decrease the
k
-dimensional volume a little bit, provided that the initial
k
-dimensional volume (or just
k
-volume) of
S
is not too big. We will either apply a suitable flow squeezing
k
-volume or the Federer-Fleming deformation (see Section
4.1
).
The rough idea to construct the flow is to run the proof of monotonicity estimate from Lemma
3.11
at each point of
S
and obtain the following dichotomy. Either the monotonicity estimate holds at a point
sK\in S
or one of the vector fields that appears in the proof of Lemma
3.11
infinitesimally squeezes the
k
-dimensional volume of
S
. In the proof, the points admitting such a field will be called heavy points.
Such fields will induce the flow deforming
S
to lower
k
-dimensional volume. To exclude the scenario where the monotonicity estimate holds at all points of
S
, we argue as in the proof of Theorem
1.14
. The proof would be literally the same if we knew that the analogue of Lemma
3.12
held for
S
or even for a fixed positive proportion of
S
.
Unfortunately, there is no reason to assume
S
satisfies a uniform monotonicity bound at small scales. For example
S
could be a very thin tube around a
(k-1)
or lower dimensional manifold. At this point we are saved by the Federer-Fleming deformation. We prove that if Lemma
3.12
fails at most points of
S
, then an application of Federer-Fleming deformation significantly decreases the
k
-dimensional volume. By repeatedly applying the squeezing flows or the Federer-Fleming deformations we make the
k
-dimensional volume of
S
small enough that a single application of Federer-Fleming collapses it into a
(k-1)
-dimensional set.

This sketch omits several important points. For example what if the flows that infinitesimally decrease the
k
-dimensional volume only do it for a very short time? We address this issue and others using the second variation formula and fine properties of the squeezing fields. The proof is constructive, and gives an explicit algorithm to deform a small
k
-varifold to a
(k-1)
-dimensional set.

8.3.
Thin part of
S
and the Federer-Fleming deformation

We keep the notation from Section
7
. In particular,
\varphi
will be a fixed smooth compactly supported spherical function with
\int_{G}\varphi(g)dg=1
. For technical reasons we choose
\varphi
so that
\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}=(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(1)>1
and
\supp\varphi\subset B_{G}(1)
. The functions
\mathfrak{u}:=S\ast\varphi\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G)
are defined as before and
\Psi(g):=\langle\mathfrak{u},\lambda_{\Gamma\backslash G}(g^{-1})\mathfrak{u}\rangle.
In this section the rectifiable varifold
S
will evolve and the same will hold for
\Psi
and other functions implicitly dependent on
S
. Still, we shall work with at most one
S
at a time so hopefully it won’t cause any confusion. We write
\tilde{S}
for the lift of
S
to
X
and
S_{0}\subset\tilde{S}
for a fundamental domain. The latter can be chosen unambiguously by intersecting
\tilde{S}
with a fixed fundamental domain of
\Gamma\backslash X
in
X
.

Using Theorem
4.8
, we choose
\delta>0
such that all compact quotients
\Gamma\backslash X
with injectivity radius at least
\varepsilon
admit an
(r,\delta)
-uniform triangulation for any small enough
r
. We choose
r_{0}>0
such that
\varphi\ast\check{\varphi}(h)\geq 1
for all
h\in B_{G}(3r_{0})
and let
\mathcal{T}
be an
(r_{0},\delta)
-uniform triangulation on
\Gamma\backslash X
. Write
\FF
for the Federer-Fleming deformation relative to
\mathcal{T}
and
S
. The constants controlling the deformation in Lemmas
4.3
,
4.4
depend only on
r_{0},\delta
, so they are independent of
\Gamma
and
S
. We will write
M=\Gamma\backslash X
for the locally symmetric manifold and
M^{(m)},M^{[m]}
for the
m
-skeleton and the set of
m
-simplices respectively.

One of the major difficulties in the proof of Theorem
1.16
compared to Theorem
1.14
is the lack of the monotonocity estimate from Lemma
3.12
.
Recall from Section
7.2
that
w_{S}(\Gamma gK,r):=\int_{G}v_{S}(ghK,r)(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dh
and
v_{S}(\Gamma gK,r):=\int_{\tilde{S}}\chi_{r}(\ff(s^{-1}g))d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)
. To lighten notation, especially inside the integrals, we will sometimes write
gK
for points in
\Gamma\backslash X=\Gamma\backslash G/K
with the understanding that we mean the coset
\Gamma gK
.
For
\eta>0
define

S_{<\eta}:=\{\Gamma sK\in S\mid w_{S}(sK,0)<\eta\},\quad S_{\geq\eta}=S-S_{<\eta}.

Lemma 8.1
.

There are constants
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon)>0,c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that either

(1)

\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{\geq\eta})\geq c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S),
or

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S))\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.4
, there exists an
\eta>0
dependent only on
\varepsilon
(because
\delta,r_{0}
are fixed depending only on
\varepsilon
), such that any point
\Gamma sK\in S
with
\mathcal{H}^{k}(B(\Gamma sK,2r_{0})\cap S)\leq\eta
is mapped to
M^{(k-1)}
under the Federer-Fleming map. Since
(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)\geq 1
for all
h\in B(3r_{0})
, we conclude that
r_{0}
-neighbourhood of any point
\Gamma sK\in S
with
w_{S}(sK,0)<\eta
must be mapped to
M^{(k-1)}.
Let
Z_{0}
be the union of all simplices of
\mathcal{T}
intersecting
S_{<\eta}
and let
Z_{1}
be the union of all simplices disjoint from
S_{<\eta}
(note that these two sets might intersect because a simplex in the first set and a simplex in the second set might share a common face).
By our previous observations, together with the fact that the diameters of all simplices in
\mathcal{T}
are bounded by
r_{0}
, we get

{\rm FF}(S\cap Z_{0})\subset M^{(k-1)},\text{ so }\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S\cap Z_{0}))=0.

On the other hand, by Lemma
4.3
,
\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S\cap Z_{1}))\leq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S\cap Z_{1})
, with
c_{0}=c_{0}(\varepsilon).
Together we get

\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S))\leq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S\cap Z_{1})\leq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{\geq\eta}).

The Lemma now follows. If
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{\geq\eta})\leq\frac{1}{2c_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
, then the second case occurs. If not, then we are in the first case, with
c=\frac{1}{2c_{0}}.

∎

Corollary 8.2
.

There are constants
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon)>0,c=c(\varepsilon)>0
such that either

(1)

\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq c\Psi(1)
and
\Psi(1)\geq\eta c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
or

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S))\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).

Proof.

By Lemma
8.1
, we can assume
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{\geq\eta})\geq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S),
with
c_{0}=c_{0}(\varepsilon)
. By making
c_{0}
smaller if necessary we can also assume
c_{0}<1
.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi(1)=

\displaystyle\int_{S}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\leq\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)+\eta\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{<\eta})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)+\eta(1-c_{0})\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).

But
\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq\eta c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
, which implies

\eta(1-c_{0})\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)\leq\eta(1-c_{0})\frac{1}{\eta c_{0}}\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

so

\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq c_{0}\Psi(1).

Similarly

\displaystyle\Psi(1)=

\displaystyle\int_{S}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\eta\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{\geq\eta})\geq\eta c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).

∎

8.4.
Heavy points and their properties

In this section we assume that
\kappa(k)>\dim X
, which happens exactly for the range of
k
allowed in Theorem
1.16
. We fix an auxiliary constant

\kappa^{\prime}:=\frac{\kappa(k)+\dim X}{2}.

The only important thing about
\kappa^{\prime}
is that it is a fixed constant strictly between
\kappa(k)
and
\dim X=m_{\alpha}+m_{2\alpha}+1.

Definition 8.3
.

A point
\Gamma K\in S
will be called
t
-heavy
if the two conditions are satisfied.

(1)

\Gamma sK\in S_{\geq\eta},

(2)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w_{S}(sK,t)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t)
and
w_{S}(sK,t+8)\leq e^{8\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|}w_{S}(sK,t)
.

The second condition can be interpreted as a bound on the ordinary and discrete derivatives of
\log w_{S}(sK,t)
at time
t.
The number
8
is not accidental, and is related to radii of the support of
\varphi
and the function
\chi_{r}(\ff)
.

The name heavy is motivated by the following geometric interpretation. At the
t
-heavy points
\Gamma sK\in S
, the varifold
S
has suspiciously large mass in a
t
-ball around
\Gamma sK
compared to
(t+8)
-ball. This property will be used to construct flows squeezing the
k
-volume of
S
.
We now adapt the proof of Theorem
1.14
from section
7.2
to show the existence of heavy points.

Lemma 8.4
.

There are constants
\alpha,r_{1}>0
, dependent only on
\varepsilon,
with the following property. Any rectifiable
k
-varifold
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
with
\injrad(\Gamma\backslash X)\geq\varepsilon
and
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)\leq\alpha\vol(\Gamma\backslash X)
satisfies either

(1)

\mathcal{H}^{k}({\rm FF}(S))\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S),

(2)

there is a
t
-heavy point
\Gamma sK\in S,t\leq r_{1}.

Proof.

By Corollary
8.2
, either (1) holds or
\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq c_{0}\Psi(1)
and
\Psi(1)\geq\eta c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
. Assume we are in the second case.
For the sake of contradiction, suppose none of the points
S_{\geq\eta}
is
t
-heavy for
0\leq t\leq r_{1}
. This means that for
\Gamma sK\in S_{\geq\eta}
and any
0<t<r_{1}
we have either
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log w_{S}(sK,t)>\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|
or
\log w_{S}(sK,t+8)-\log w_{S}(sK,t)>8\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|
. Since
\log w_{S}(sK,t)
is non-decreasing, it easily implies
\log w_{S}(sK,r_{1})\geq\log w_{S}(sK,0)+\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|(r_{1}-8).
By (
7.8
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{G}\chi_{r}(\ff(g))\Psi(g)dg\geq

\displaystyle\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,r)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle c_{1}e^{\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|r_{1}}\int_{S_{\geq\eta}}w_{S}(sK,0)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(sK)\geq c_{1}e^{\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|r_{1}}\Psi(1).

Now arguing exactly as in the proof of Theorem
1.14
we arrive at

\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\geq\Psi(1)(c_{2}e^{(\kappa^{\prime}-m_{\alpha}-2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r_{1}}-c_{3}e^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r_{1}}(1+r_{1})),

with
c_{1},c_{2}
depending only on
\varepsilon
. We put

A(r_{1}):=c_{2}e^{(\kappa^{\prime}-m_{\alpha}-2m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r_{1}}-c_{3}e^{(1-m_{2\alpha})\|\alpha\|r_{1}}(1+r_{1}).

Since
\kappa^{\prime}>\dim X=1+m_{\alpha}+m_{2\alpha}
, for
r_{1}
big enough
A(r_{1})>0
(how big depending only on
\varepsilon
). Then,

\frac{\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)^{2}}{\vol(\Gamma\backslash G)}\geq\Psi(1)A(r_{1})\geq\eta c_{0}A(r_{1})\mathcal{H}^{k}(S).

If we took
\alpha<\eta c_{0}A(r_{1})
this would be a contradiction with our assumption. Therefore, for the right choice of
\alpha,r_{1}
the dichotomy holds.
∎

In the next lemma, we unwind the proof of Lemma
3.11
to show that certain vector fields “collapsing" the space towards a
t
-heavy point will squeeze the
k
-volume of
S
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
be a rectifiable
k
-varifold and
\injrad(\Gamma\backslash X)\geq\varepsilon.
Suppose
S
contains a
t
-heavy point
\Gamma sK
. Then, there is a smooth vector field
Y
on
\Gamma\backslash X
with the associated flow
\Phi_{v}\colon(\Gamma\backslash X)\to(\Gamma\backslash X),\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\Phi_{v}(x)=Y(\Phi_{v}(x)),x\in\Gamma\backslash X
satisfying

(1)

\|Y\|,\|\nabla Y\|\leq c=c(\varepsilon)
uniformly on
\Gamma\backslash X
.

(2)

Y
is supported on
\Gamma B(sK,t+3)
(seen as a subset of
\Gamma\backslash X
).

(3)

\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))|_{v=0}\leq-\left(\frac{\kappa(k)-\dim X}{2}\right)w_{S}(sK,t).

Proof.

We start by defining fields
Y^{t}_{gK},gK\in X,t>0
. We’ll write
l(g)F(h):=F(g^{-1}h)
for any function
F
on
X
. The action naturally extends to vector fields on
X
, in such a way that
l(g)F_{1}\grad(l(g)F_{2})=l(g)(F_{1}\grad F_{2})
for any
F_{1},F_{2}\in C^{1}(X)
. Recall the definition of the function
\ff
that smoothly approximates the distance function (
6.1
). Put

Y^{t}_{gK}:=-l(g)\left(\chi_{t}(\ff)\grad\ff\right).

This is a smooth vector field on
X
supported on
B(gK,t+1)
, satisfying
\|Y_{gK}^{t}\|,

\|\nabla Y_{gK}^{t}\|\leq c_{0}
uniformly on
X
. One can imagine that this is a field pulling the interior of the
(t+1)
-ball around
gK\in X
towards the center at roughly constant unit speed, suitably smoothed at
gX
and the boundary of the ball.
Let
\Gamma sK\in S
be the
t
-heavy point. Define

Y:=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)Y^{t}_{\gamma shK}dh.

The field is a left
\Gamma
invariant smooth vector field on
X
so it descends to a smooth vector field on
\Gamma\backslash X
. For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
the field
\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)Y^{t}_{\gamma sh}dh
is supported on
B(\gamma sK,t+3)
, since
\supp(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})\subset B(2).
For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the field
\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)Y^{t}_{\gamma shK}
is a convex combination of the fields of type
Y^{t}_{gK}
so its norm and the norms of its first two covariant derivatives are still bounded by
c_{0}.
The sum over
\gamma
is locally finite, with the number of non-vanishing summands over a point bounded uniformly in terms of
\varepsilon.
It follows that the field
Y
satisfies the conditions (1) and (2). It remains to verify (3).

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))|_{v=0}=

\displaystyle\int_{S}{\rm div}_{S}(Y)(uK)d\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{S_{0}}\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h){\rm div}_{S}(Y^{t}_{\gamma sh})(uK)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h){\rm div}_{S}(Y^{t}_{sh})(uK)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

We now use the formula for divergence
{\rm div}_{S}Y_{sh}^{t}
from Lemma
3.11
, and repeat the computation therein to upper bound the last expression.

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}\int_{G}(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)

\displaystyle{\rm div}_{S}(Y^{t}_{sh})dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|\int_{\tilde{S}}\int_{G}\chi_{t}(\ff(u^{-1}sh))(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}\int_{G}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\chi_{t}(\ff(u^{-1}sh))(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(uK)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\kappa(k)\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t)+\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w_{S}(sK,t)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\kappa(k)-\dim X}{2}\right)\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t).

The last inequality used the assumption that
\Gamma sK
was
t
-heavy:
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w_{S}(sK,t)\leq\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t)=\frac{(\text{dim}(X)+\kappa)}{2}\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t).

∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
Y
be a smooth vector field on
\Gamma\backslash X
, supported on a closed set
B
, satisfying
\|Y\|,\|\nabla Y\|,\|\nabla^{2}Y\|\leq A
uniformly on
\Gamma\backslash X
. Let
\Phi_{v}
be the flow associated to
Y
. For any rectifiable
k
-varifold
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X

\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial v)^{2}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))\right|\leq c\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S)\cap B).

Proof.

Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
be
C^{1}
-vector fields forming an orthonormal basis of
T_{\Gamma sK}S
at
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)
-almost every point of
S
. Write
R
for the curvature tensor of
\Gamma\backslash X
. By the second variation formula (see
[
CM11
, (1.142)]
or
[
MM16
, Theorem 6.1]
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial v)^{2}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))|_{v=0}=

\displaystyle\int_{S}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\|\nabla_{e_{i}}^{\perp}Y\|^{2}-{\rm div}_{S}\nabla_{Y}Y-\sum_{i=1}^{k}R(Y,e_{i},Y,e_{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}Y,e_{i}\rangle\right)^{2}-\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\langle\nabla_{e_{j}}Y,e_{i}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}Y,e_{j}\rangle\right)d\mathcal{H}^{k}.

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial v)^{2}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))|_{v=0}\right|\leq

\displaystyle\int_{S\cap B}\left(kA^{2}+kA^{2}+c_{0}kA^{2}+k^{2}A^{2}+k^{2}A^{2}\right)d\mathcal{H}^{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\mathcal{H}^{k}(S\cap B).

Same computation yields the bound at all times
v
.
∎

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
Y
be a smooth vector field on
\Gamma\backslash X
, supported on a closed set
B
, satisfying
\|Y\|\leq A
uniformly on
\Gamma\backslash X
. Let
\Phi_{v}
be the flow associated to
Y
. For any rectifiable
k
-varifold
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
and any
v_{0}>0

\mathcal{H}^{k+1}\left(\bigcup_{v\in[0,v_{0}]}\Phi_{v}(S)\right)\leq A\int_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S)\cap B)dv.

Proof.

For each fixed
v
,
\Phi_{v}
restricted to
S
is a diffeomorphism on the subset of regular points. Denote by
g_{v}
the induced metric on
\Phi_{v}(S)
. We can define a metric on
S\times[0,v_{0}]
by
\Phi_{v}(s)^{*}g_{v}+Adv^{2}
at each point
(s,v)
. The volume of
S\times[0,v_{0}]
in this metric is equal to
A\int_{0}^{v_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S)\cap B)dv.
Then since the Jacobian of the map
\Phi\colon S\times[0,v_{0}]\rightarrow\Gamma\backslash X
is pointwise at most one, this proves the lemma.

∎

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
S\subset\Gamma\backslash X
be a rectifiable
k
-varifold and
\injrad(\Gamma\backslash X)\geq\varepsilon.
Suppose
S
contains a
t
-heavy point
sK
. Let
Y
be the vector field afforded by Lemma
8.5
and let
\Phi_{v}
be the associated flow on
\Gamma\backslash X
. Then there is a time
v_{0}=v_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
and
\tau_{0}=\tau_{0}(\varepsilon)>0
such that

(1)

\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v_{0}}(S))\leq\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)-\tau_{0},

(2)

\mathcal{H}^{k+1}(\bigcup_{v\in[0,v_{0}]}\Phi_{v}(S))\leq c(\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)-\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v_{0}}(S))),
with
c=c(\varepsilon)>0.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.5
,
\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))|_{v=0}\leq-\left(\frac{\kappa(k)-\dim X}{2}\right)\|\alpha\|w_{S}(sK,t).
Let
B:=\Gamma B(sK,t+3)\subset\Gamma\backslash X
.
Put
v_{1}=\sup\{v\geq 0\mid\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))<0\}.
By definition,
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))
will be decreasing for
v\in[0,v_{1}]
. We know that
Y
is supported on
B
so the flow fixes the outside of
B
. In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S)\cap B)
is also decreasing on
[0,v_{1}]
.

By Lemma
8.6
, for all
v\in[0,v_{1}]

\left|\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial v)^{2}}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S))\right|\leq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Phi_{v}(S)\cap B)\leq c_{0}\mathcal{H}^{k}(S\cap B).

By the definition of a
t
-heavy point,
w_{S}(sK,t+8)\leq e^{8\kappa^{\prime}\|\alpha\|}w_{S}(sK,t)
. If we unfold the definition of
w_{S}(sK,t+8)
, we see that

\displaystyle w_{S}(sK,t+8)=

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{S}}\int_{G}\chi_{t+8}(\ff(u^{-1}sh))(\varphi\ast\check{\varphi})(h)dhd\mathcal{H}^{k}(S)

\displaystyle\geq
